## Areas of Clinical Focus (118)

### 97. nce-s-clf-001 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · easy · In review

Under DSM-5-TR, what is the minimum duration for the symptoms of a major depressive episode?

**A. [ ]** One week
> One week is the minimum duration for a manic episode, not a major depressive episode.

**B. [✔]** Two weeks
> A major depressive episode requires five or more symptoms present during the same 2-week period.

**C. [ ]** One month
> One month is not the major depressive episode threshold; it is used for other diagnoses, such as the active phase of schizophrenia.

**D. [ ]** Six months
> Six months is the duration for generalized anxiety disorder, not a major depressive episode.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR requires at least five of nine symptoms, including depressed mood or loss of interest or pleasure, present nearly every day during the same 2-week period and representing a change from prior functioning. Knowing duration thresholds helps counselors tell a depressive episode apart from shorter, situational low mood.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Major Depressive Disorder, Criterion A

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 98. nce-s-clf-002 · Assessment and Testing · depressive disorders · medium · In review

A 41-year-old client reports feeling down more days than not for the past 3 years, with low energy, poor appetite, and hopelessness. She cannot recall a stretch of more than a few weeks without these symptoms and has never had a manic or hypomanic episode. Full criteria for a major depressive episode have not been met. Which diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [ ]** Cyclothymic disorder
> Cyclothymic disorder requires numerous periods of hypomanic symptoms alongside depressive symptoms; she reports no elevated mood.

**B. [ ]** Adjustment disorder with depressed mood
> Adjustment disorder follows an identifiable stressor and does not persist more than 6 months after the stressor ends; this is a chronic 3-year pattern.

**C. [ ]** Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder
> DMDD is a childhood diagnosis (first given between ages 6 and 18, onset before 10) marked by severe temper outbursts.

**D. [✔]** Persistent depressive disorder
> Depressed mood more days than not for at least 2 years in an adult, with two or more associated symptoms and no symptom-free period longer than 2 months, fits persistent depressive disorder.

**Rationale:** Persistent depressive disorder requires depressed mood for most of the day, more days than not, for at least 2 years in adults (1 year in children and adolescents), plus at least two associated symptoms such as appetite change, low energy, or hopelessness. During that period the person has not been without symptoms for more than 2 months at a time, and there has never been a manic or hypomanic episode.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Persistent Depressive Disorder, Criteria A–C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 99. nce-s-clf-003 · Human Growth and Development · depressive disorders · hard · In review

A 15-year-old has shown irritable mood on most days for the past 14 months, along with low self-esteem and poor concentration. He has not gone more than a few weeks without these symptoms, has no history of severe temper outbursts, and has never met criteria for a manic or hypomanic episode. Which statement is MOST accurate?

**A. [✔]** Criteria for persistent depressive disorder can be met at this point
> For children and adolescents, mood may be irritable and the duration requirement is 1 year, so 14 months is sufficient.

**B. [ ]** Persistent depressive disorder requires 2 full years in any age group
> The 2-year requirement applies to adults; DSM-5-TR shortens it to 1 year for children and adolescents.

**C. [ ]** Irritable rather than sad mood rules out a depressive disorder diagnosis
> DSM-5-TR allows irritable mood to satisfy the mood criterion for depressive disorders in children and adolescents.

**D. [ ]** Disruptive mood dysregulation disorder is the required diagnosis here
> DMDD requires severe, recurrent temper outbursts with onset before age 10, which this teen does not show.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR modifies persistent depressive disorder for youth in two ways: mood can be irritable rather than depressed, and the minimum duration is 1 year instead of 2. Counselors working with adolescents should recognize that chronic irritability can be a depressive presentation rather than simply a behavior problem.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Persistent Depressive Disorder, Criterion A (children and adolescents)

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 100. nce-s-clf-004 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · easy · In review

Unless hospitalization is required, what is the minimum duration of a manic episode in DSM-5-TR?

**A. [ ]** Four consecutive days
> Four consecutive days is the minimum for a hypomanic episode, not a manic episode.

**B. [ ]** Two weeks
> Two weeks is the duration requirement for a major depressive episode.

**C. [✔]** One week
> A manic episode lasts at least 1 week and is present most of the day, nearly every day, or any duration if hospitalization is needed.

**D. [ ]** One month
> One month is not a mood episode threshold; it is too long for mania.

**Rationale:** A manic episode is a distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood plus increased activity or energy lasting at least 1 week (any duration if hospitalization is necessary). A hypomanic episode needs only 4 consecutive days and does not cause marked impairment or require hospitalization.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Bipolar I Disorder, Manic Episode Criterion A

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 101. nce-s-clf-005 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · medium · In review

A 30-year-old client has had two major depressive episodes. Last spring she had 5 days of unusually elevated mood, less need for sleep, and increased productivity that friends noticed but that did not impair her work or require hospitalization. She has never had psychotic symptoms or a longer elevated period. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?

**A. [✔]** Bipolar II disorder
> At least one hypomanic episode (4+ days, no marked impairment) plus at least one major depressive episode, with no history of mania, defines bipolar II disorder.

**B. [ ]** Bipolar I disorder
> Bipolar I requires at least one full manic episode; her elevated period was hypomanic.

**C. [ ]** Cyclothymic disorder
> Cyclothymic disorder requires symptoms that never meet full criteria for a major depressive episode; she has had two.

**D. [ ]** Major depressive disorder
> A history of a hypomanic episode excludes a diagnosis of major depressive disorder.

**Rationale:** Bipolar II disorder requires at least one hypomanic episode and at least one major depressive episode, with no lifetime manic episode. Hypomania lasts at least 4 consecutive days, is observable by others, and is not severe enough to cause marked impairment or hospitalization. Screening for past hypomania is essential before treating depression as unipolar.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Bipolar II Disorder, Criteria A–B; Hypomanic Episode

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 102. nce-s-clf-006 · Assessment and Testing · bipolar disorders · hard · In review

A 27-year-old with no prior psychiatric history develops grandiosity, pressured speech, almost no sleep, and reckless spending. On the third day he is psychiatrically hospitalized because his behavior has become dangerous. Substances and medical causes are ruled out. Which statement is MOST accurate?

**A. [ ]** The episode is hypomanic because it lasted fewer than 4 days
> Hypomania itself requires at least 4 days and, by definition, does not require hospitalization.

**B. [ ]** No bipolar diagnosis applies until symptoms reach 1 full week
> The 1-week requirement is waived when hospitalization is necessary.

**C. [ ]** Bipolar I also requires a prior major depressive episode on record
> A single manic episode is sufficient for bipolar I; a depressive episode is common but not required.

**D. [✔]** The episode is manic since hospitalization waives the duration
> DSM-5-TR counts a manic episode of any duration if hospitalization is necessary, so bipolar I disorder can be diagnosed.

**Rationale:** The 1-week duration for a manic episode applies unless hospitalization is necessary, in which case any duration qualifies. Bipolar I disorder requires only one lifetime manic episode; major depressive episodes are common but are not required for the diagnosis.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Bipolar I Disorder, Manic Episode Criterion A and Criterion for Bipolar I

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 103. nce-s-clf-007 · Assessment and Testing · anxiety disorders · easy · In review

For a diagnosis of generalized anxiety disorder, excessive anxiety and worry must occur more days than not for at least how long?

**A. [ ]** 1 month
> One month is the post-attack worry period for panic disorder, not the GAD duration.

**B. [✔]** 6 months
> GAD requires excessive anxiety and worry more days than not for at least 6 months.

**C. [ ]** 3 months
> Three months is not the GAD threshold.

**D. [ ]** 2 years
> Two years is the adult duration for persistent depressive disorder.

**Rationale:** Generalized anxiety disorder involves excessive, hard-to-control worry about a number of events or activities, occurring more days than not for at least 6 months, with three or more associated symptoms in adults (only one in children), such as restlessness, fatigue, or sleep disturbance.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Generalized Anxiety Disorder, Criterion A

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 104. nce-s-clf-008 · Assessment and Testing · anxiety disorders · medium · In review

A 36-year-old client has had several sudden, unexpected surges of intense fear with a pounding heart, shortness of breath, and dizziness. For the past 2 months he has worried constantly about having another attack and has stopped exercising because a racing heart might trigger one. Medical causes have been ruled out. Which diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [✔]** Panic disorder
> Recurrent unexpected panic attacks followed by at least 1 month of persistent worry or maladaptive behavior change define panic disorder.

**B. [ ]** Agoraphobia
> Agoraphobia requires fear or avoidance in at least two situations such as crowds or public transit; his avoidance centers on bodily sensations.

**C. [ ]** Illness anxiety disorder
> Illness anxiety centers on a preoccupation with having a serious illness, not on recurrent unexpected panic attacks.

**D. [ ]** Specific phobia, situational
> Specific phobia involves fear cued by a particular object or situation, whereas these attacks are unexpected.

**Rationale:** Panic disorder requires recurrent unexpected panic attacks, with at least one attack followed by 1 month or more of persistent concern about further attacks or their consequences, or a significant maladaptive change in behavior such as avoiding exercise. Interoceptive avoidance like this is a common target of cognitive behavioral treatment.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Panic Disorder, Criteria A–B · Barlow PCT — Interoceptive avoidance and exposure

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 105. nce-s-clf-009 · Assessment and Testing · trauma- and stressor-related disorders · medium · In review

Ten days after a serious highway collision, a 24-year-old reports intrusive memories, nightmares, feeling as if the world is unreal, avoiding driving, and trouble sleeping and concentrating. The symptoms began right after the crash and are impairing her work. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate at this time?

**A. [ ]** Posttraumatic stress disorder
> PTSD requires symptoms lasting more than 1 month; only 10 days have passed.

**B. [ ]** Adjustment disorder with anxiety
> When symptoms meet criteria for a trauma-specific disorder, adjustment disorder is not the best fit.

**C. [✔]** Acute stress disorder
> Acute stress disorder applies when trauma-related symptoms last from 3 days to 1 month after exposure.

**D. [ ]** Generalized anxiety disorder
> GAD involves diffuse worry for at least 6 months and is not tied to a traumatic event.

**Rationale:** Acute stress disorder is diagnosed when nine or more symptoms across intrusion, negative mood, dissociation, avoidance, and arousal last from 3 days to 1 month after trauma exposure. If symptoms persist beyond 1 month and meet PTSD criteria, the diagnosis is changed to PTSD.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Acute Stress Disorder, Criteria B–C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 106. nce-s-clf-010 · Assessment and Testing · trauma- and stressor-related disorders · hard · In review

A firefighter developed some sleep problems and irritability soon after a fatal fire. Eight months later, after more symptoms emerged, she meets full criteria for PTSD for the first time. She does not report depersonalization or derealization. Which specifier applies?

**A. [ ]** With dissociative symptoms
> The dissociative specifier requires persistent depersonalization or derealization, which she denies.

**B. [ ]** With panic attacks
> The with-panic-attacks specifier requires panic attacks alongside the disorder, and none are described here.

**C. [ ]** With acute onset
> DSM-5-TR PTSD has no acute-onset specifier; the DSM-IV acute and chronic specifiers were removed.

**D. [✔]** With delayed expression
> This specifier is used when full criteria are not met until at least 6 months after the event, even if some symptoms began earlier.

**Rationale:** In DSM-5-TR, PTSD "with delayed expression" is specified when full diagnostic criteria are not met until at least 6 months after the event, although the onset and expression of some symptoms may be immediate. The other PTSD specifier is "with dissociative symptoms" (depersonalization or derealization).

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Posttraumatic Stress Disorder, specifiers

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 107. nce-s-clf-011 · Assessment and Testing · obsessive-compulsive and related disorders · medium · In review

A client with contamination obsessions washes his hands for hours daily. He says his family will probably become seriously ill if he stops washing, although he admits he could be wrong. Which DSM-5-TR insight specifier is MOST accurate?

**A. [ ]** With good or fair insight
> Good or fair insight means the person recognizes the beliefs are definitely or probably not true.

**B. [✔]** With poor insight
> Poor insight applies when the person thinks the OCD beliefs are probably true.

**C. [ ]** With absent insight
> Absent insight/delusional beliefs applies when the person is completely convinced the beliefs are true; he admits doubt.

**D. [ ]** With tic-related features
> The tic-related specifier concerns a current or past tic disorder, not the degree of insight.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR rates insight in OCD on three levels: good or fair (beliefs definitely or probably not true), poor (beliefs probably true), and absent insight/delusional beliefs (completely convinced the beliefs are true). A separate "tic-related" specifier notes a current or past tic disorder.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Obsessive-Compulsive Disorder, insight specifiers

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 108. nce-s-clf-012 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · obsessive-compulsive and related disorders · easy · In review

Which psychological intervention has the strongest evidence as a first-line treatment for obsessive-compulsive disorder?

**A. [ ]** Insight-oriented psychodynamic therapy
> Psychodynamic therapy lacks the evidence base that exposure-based CBT has for OCD.

**B. [ ]** Thought stopping with reassurance
> Reassurance acts as a compulsion and maintains OCD; thought stopping tends to backfire.

**C. [✔]** Exposure and response prevention
> ERP, a form of CBT, exposes clients to feared cues while they refrain from compulsions and is the first-line psychological treatment.

**D. [ ]** Nondirective supportive counseling
> Supportive counseling alone does not target the obsession–compulsion cycle.

**Rationale:** Exposure and response prevention has clients face feared thoughts, images, or situations while resisting compulsions, allowing anxiety to decline without the ritual and weakening the negative reinforcement that maintains OCD. Reassurance-giving by the counselor or family is itself a compulsion to be reduced.

**References:** NICE guidelines — CG31 Obsessive-compulsive disorder and body dysmorphic disorder: CBT including ERP

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 109. nce-s-clf-013 · Assessment and Testing · substance use and addictive disorders · medium · In review

Over the past 12 months, a client has met five of the eleven DSM-5-TR criteria for alcohol use disorder. Which severity specifier applies?

**A. [ ]** Mild
> Mild applies when two or three criteria are met.

**B. [ ]** Severe
> Severe applies when six or more criteria are met.

**C. [✔]** Moderate
> Moderate applies when four or five criteria are met.

**D. [ ]** Subthreshold
> Two criteria within 12 months are enough for a diagnosis, so five is well above threshold.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR substance use disorders require at least two of eleven criteria within a 12-month period. Severity is specified as mild (2–3 criteria), moderate (4–5), or severe (6 or more). Severity guides level-of-care decisions alongside a dimensional assessment such as the ASAM Criteria.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Alcohol Use Disorder, severity specifiers

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 110. nce-s-clf-014 · Assessment and Testing · substance use and addictive disorders · medium · In review

A 52-year-old takes a prescribed opioid exactly as directed for chronic back pain under a physician's care. She reports needing a higher dose than at first and feeling withdrawal symptoms if a dose is late. No other use disorder criteria are present. How should the counselor understand these symptoms?

**A. [✔]** They are not counted toward an opioid use disorder
> Tolerance and withdrawal are not counted when the opioid is taken solely under appropriate medical supervision.

**B. [ ]** They meet criteria for mild opioid use disorder
> Two counted criteria would be mild, but these two are excluded in supervised medical use.

**C. [ ]** They confirm a severe opioid use disorder is present
> Physiological dependence alone does not establish any use disorder, let alone a severe one.

**D. [ ]** They point to opioid use disorder in remission
> Remission requires a prior full use disorder, which has not been established.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR notes that tolerance and withdrawal are expected physiological adaptations when medications such as opioids, sedatives, or stimulants are taken as prescribed under medical supervision, and they are not counted toward a use disorder in that situation. A diagnosis requires other evidence of problematic use, such as craving or loss of control.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Opioid Use Disorder, Criteria 10–11 note on medical supervision

**⚑ Check:** The cited 'Criteria 10-11 note' detail is the writer's wording of where the DSM-5-TR note sits.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

### 111. nce-s-clf-015 · Human Growth and Development · neurodevelopmental disorders · easy · In review

Under DSM-5-TR, several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms of ADHD must have been present before what age?

**A. [ ]** Age 7
> Onset before age 7 was the DSM-IV standard; DSM-5 raised it to 12.

**B. [✔]** Age 12
> DSM-5-TR requires several symptoms to have been present before age 12.

**C. [ ]** Age 16
> Age 16 is not a criterion; age 17 marks where the adult symptom count of five applies.

**D. [ ]** Age 18
> Age 18 is not the ADHD onset threshold.

**Rationale:** ADHD requires several inattentive or hyperactive-impulsive symptoms before age 12, present in two or more settings, with clear interference in functioning. Children need six symptoms in a domain; adolescents 17 and older and adults need five. Adult assessment therefore includes a developmental history.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder, Criteria A and C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 112. nce-s-clf-016 · Human Growth and Development · neurodevelopmental disorders · hard · In review

A 7-year-old struggles to take turns in conversation, misses jokes and hints, and speaks to teachers the same way he speaks to peers. A counselor is weighing autism spectrum disorder against social (pragmatic) communication disorder. Which finding would MOST support autism spectrum disorder?

**A. [ ]** Late onset of first spoken words
> Language delay can occur in many conditions and is not a defining criterion that separates the two.

**B. [ ]** Trouble using language socially
> Difficulty with social use of language is central to both disorders, so it does not distinguish them.

**C. [ ]** Below-average intellectual ability
> Intellectual impairment is a possible specifier for ASD but is neither required nor distinguishing.

**D. [✔]** Restricted, repetitive behaviors
> ASD requires restricted, repetitive patterns of behavior, interests, or activities; their presence rules out SCD.

**Rationale:** Both ASD and social (pragmatic) communication disorder involve deficits in social communication. ASD also requires at least two kinds of restricted, repetitive patterns of behavior, interests, or activities (such as rigid routines, intense interests, or sensory differences). SCD is diagnosed only when such patterns are absent, past or present.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Social (Pragmatic) Communication Disorder, Criterion D; Autism Spectrum Disorder, Criterion B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 113. nce-s-clf-017 · Assessment and Testing · schizophrenia spectrum disorders · medium · In review

A 22-year-old college student has had auditory hallucinations, persecutory delusions, and disorganized speech for about 3 months, including any early warning signs. There have been no mood episodes, and substance and medical causes are ruled out. Which diagnosis is MOST appropriate?

**A. [✔]** Schizophreniform disorder
> Schizophreniform disorder has schizophrenia-like symptoms lasting at least 1 month but less than 6 months.

**B. [ ]** Schizophrenia
> Schizophrenia requires continuous signs of disturbance for at least 6 months.

**C. [ ]** Brief psychotic disorder
> Brief psychotic disorder lasts at least 1 day but less than 1 month, with full return to functioning.

**D. [ ]** Delusional disorder
> Delusional disorder involves delusions without prominent hallucinations or disorganized speech.

**Rationale:** The schizophrenia spectrum is partly sorted by duration: brief psychotic disorder lasts 1 day to less than 1 month; schizophreniform disorder lasts 1 month to less than 6 months; schizophrenia requires at least 6 months of disturbance, including at least 1 month of active-phase symptoms.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Schizophreniform Disorder, Criteria A–B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 114. nce-s-clf-018 · Assessment and Testing · schizophrenia spectrum disorders · hard · In review

A 26-year-old has had 5 weeks of prominent hallucinations and delusions. His family reports that for about 6 months before that, he withdrew from friends, showed flat affect, and voiced odd beliefs. No mood episodes have occurred, and substance and medical causes are excluded. Which diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [ ]** Schizophreniform disorder
> The prodromal period counts toward duration, so the total exceeds the less-than-6-months limit for schizophreniform disorder.

**B. [✔]** Schizophrenia
> More than 1 month of active symptoms plus a prodromal period gives over 6 months of continuous disturbance.

**C. [ ]** Brief psychotic disorder
> Brief psychotic disorder lasts less than 1 month in total; the active phase alone is longer.

**D. [ ]** Schizoaffective disorder
> Schizoaffective disorder requires a major mood episode concurrent with psychotic symptoms; none occurred.

**Rationale:** Schizophrenia requires continuous signs of disturbance for at least 6 months, which must include at least 1 month of active-phase symptoms and may include prodromal or residual periods with negative symptoms or attenuated positive symptoms. Collateral history from family is often essential for establishing the prodromal phase.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Schizophrenia, Criterion C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 115. nce-s-clf-019 · Assessment and Testing · personality disorders · easy · In review

Which personality disorder belongs to DSM-5-TR Cluster C (the anxious or fearful cluster)?

**A. [ ]** Schizotypal personality disorder
> Schizotypal is in Cluster A, the odd or eccentric cluster.

**B. [ ]** Histrionic personality disorder
> Histrionic is in Cluster B, the dramatic, emotional, or erratic cluster.

**C. [✔]** Avoidant personality disorder
> Avoidant personality disorder is in Cluster C with dependent and obsessive-compulsive personality disorders.

**D. [ ]** Paranoid personality disorder
> Paranoid is in Cluster A with schizoid and schizotypal.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR groups personality disorders into three clusters: A (paranoid, schizoid, schizotypal) for odd or eccentric patterns; B (antisocial, borderline, histrionic, narcissistic) for dramatic or erratic patterns; and C (avoidant, dependent, obsessive-compulsive) for anxious or fearful patterns.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Personality Disorders, cluster groupings

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 116. nce-s-clf-020 · Assessment and Testing · personality disorders · medium · In review

A 20-year-old shows a pervasive pattern of deceitfulness, impulsivity, repeated fights, and lack of remorse since age 15. To diagnose antisocial personality disorder, which additional finding does DSM-5-TR require?

**A. [ ]** A documented history of arrest or jail
> Legal involvement is common but not required; failure to conform to social norms can be shown in other ways.

**B. [ ]** No co-occurring substance use disorder
> Substance use disorders frequently co-occur and do not rule out antisocial personality disorder.

**C. [ ]** Onset of the pattern after age 18 only
> The pattern must be evident since age 15; onset is not restricted to adulthood.

**D. [✔]** Evidence of conduct disorder before 15
> DSM-5-TR requires evidence of conduct disorder with onset before age 15, and the person must be at least 18.

**Rationale:** Antisocial personality disorder requires a pervasive pattern of disregard for and violation of the rights of others occurring since age 15, a current age of at least 18, and evidence of conduct disorder with onset before age 15. The behavior must not occur only during schizophrenia or bipolar disorder.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Antisocial Personality Disorder, Criteria B–C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

### 117. nce-s-clf-021 · Assessment and Testing · eating disorders · medium · In review

A 33-year-old describes episodes, about twice a week for 6 months, of eating far more than most people would in a short period while feeling unable to stop. She eats rapidly, eats alone out of embarrassment, and feels disgusted afterward. She does not vomit, fast, misuse laxatives, or exercise to compensate. Which diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [ ]** Bulimia nervosa
> Bulimia nervosa requires recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors, which she does not use.

**B. [✔]** Binge-eating disorder
> Recurrent binges with loss of control and marked distress, at least weekly for 3 months, without regular compensatory behavior, fit binge-eating disorder.

**C. [ ]** ARFID
> ARFID involves restricted intake, not binge episodes.

**D. [ ]** Anorexia nervosa, binge/purge type
> Anorexia nervosa requires restriction leading to significantly low body weight and fear of weight gain, none of which is described, and she does not purge.

**Rationale:** Binge-eating disorder requires recurrent binge episodes with a sense of loss of control, three or more associated features (such as eating rapidly, eating alone from embarrassment, or feeling disgusted afterward), marked distress, and a frequency of at least once a week for 3 months. Unlike bulimia nervosa, there are no recurrent compensatory behaviors.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Binge-Eating Disorder, Criteria A–E

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 118. nce-s-clf-022 · Human Growth and Development · eating disorders · medium · In review

Since choking on a piece of food 4 months ago, a 10-year-old refuses most solid foods, eats only liquids and soft foods, and has lost weight. She expresses no concern about body shape or size and wants to be "back to normal." Which diagnosis is MOST likely?

**A. [ ]** Anorexia nervosa
> Anorexia nervosa involves fear of weight gain or disturbed body image, which she does not show.

**B. [ ]** Rumination disorder
> Rumination disorder involves repeated regurgitation of food, not avoidance after choking.

**C. [✔]** ARFID
> ARFID covers restriction driven by fear of aversive consequences such as choking, without body image disturbance.

**D. [ ]** Pica
> Pica involves persistent eating of nonnutritive, nonfood substances.

**Rationale:** Avoidant/restrictive food intake disorder involves restricted eating that leads to weight loss, nutritional deficiency, or impaired functioning, motivated by lack of interest in food, sensory aversions, or fear of aversive consequences such as choking or vomiting. It is distinguished from anorexia nervosa by the absence of fear of weight gain or disturbed body image.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Avoidant/Restrictive Food Intake Disorder, Criteria A and C

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 119. nce-s-clf-023 · Assessment and Testing · neurocognitive disorders · medium · In review

A 74-year-old retired teacher and her daughter both report a gradual decline in her memory over the past year. Testing shows a modest decline from her prior level. She still manages her own bills, medications, and shopping, although these tasks now take more effort and reminder lists. Which diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [ ]** Major neurocognitive disorder
> Major NCD requires substantial decline that interferes with independence in everyday activities.

**B. [ ]** Delirium
> Delirium has an acute onset with fluctuating attention and awareness, not a gradual year-long decline.

**C. [✔]** Mild neurocognitive disorder
> Modest documented decline that does not interfere with independence, even if compensation is needed, fits mild NCD.

**D. [ ]** Dissociative amnesia
> Dissociative amnesia involves inability to recall autobiographical information, usually traumatic, not a gradual cognitive decline.

**Rationale:** Mild and major neurocognitive disorder differ chiefly in functional impact. Mild NCD shows modest decline from a previous level, supported by concern and testing, without loss of independence in everyday activities, though greater effort or compensatory strategies may be needed. Major NCD involves significant decline that interferes with independence.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Mild Neurocognitive Disorder, Criteria A–B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 120. nce-s-clf-024 · Assessment and Testing · neurocognitive disorders · medium · In review

An 81-year-old hospitalized for a urinary tract infection became confused over 2 days. Staff note she is alert one hour and disoriented the next. A counselor on the unit is asked to help distinguish delirium from major neurocognitive disorder. Which feature MOST points to delirium?

**A. [✔]** Acute onset with fluctuating attention
> Delirium develops over hours to days and fluctuates in severity, with disturbed attention and awareness.

**B. [ ]** Memory impairment found on screening
> Memory deficits occur in both delirium and major NCD, so they do not distinguish them.

**C. [ ]** Onset of symptoms after the age of 65
> Both conditions are more common in older adults; age does not separate them.

**D. [ ]** Presence of vivid visual hallucinations
> Visual hallucinations can occur in delirium and in major NCD, notably with Lewy bodies.

**Rationale:** Delirium is a disturbance in attention and awareness that develops over a short period (hours to a few days), represents a change from baseline, and tends to fluctuate during the day. It often has a medical cause, such as infection or medication effects, and requires prompt medical evaluation.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Delirium, Criteria A–B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

### 121. nce-s-clf-025 · Assessment and Testing · grief and loss · hard · In review

A 58-year-old whose wife died 7 months ago reports intense yearning for her nearly every day, feeling that part of himself has died, and difficulty re-engaging with friends. His grief is far beyond what is expected in his community and is impairing his work. Which statement about prolonged grief disorder is MOST accurate?

**A. [ ]** It can be diagnosed now because symptoms have lasted over 6 months
> Six months is the threshold for children and adolescents, not adults.

**B. [✔]** It cannot yet be diagnosed because 12 months have not yet passed
> For adults, DSM-5-TR requires at least 12 months since the death.

**C. [ ]** It is not in DSM-5-TR, so his grief is coded only as a Z code
> Prolonged grief disorder was added to the trauma- and stressor-related disorders in DSM-5-TR; uncomplicated bereavement is the Z code.

**D. [ ]** It is ruled out, and major depression must be diagnosed instead
> Nothing here establishes a major depressive episode; yearning and identity disruption are grief-specific.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR added prolonged grief disorder to the trauma- and stressor-related disorders. It requires that at least 12 months have passed since the death for adults (6 months for children and adolescents), with intense yearning or preoccupation nearly every day for at least the last month, plus at least three additional symptoms such as identity disruption or emotional numbness.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Prolonged Grief Disorder, Criterion A

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 122. nce-s-clf-026 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · grief and loss · easy · In review

Six weeks after his mother died, a client reports sadness, poor sleep, and crying spells. Which feature is MORE typical of grief than of a major depressive episode?

**A. [ ]** Pervasive worthlessness and self-loathing
> Pervasive worthlessness and self-loathing are more typical of a major depressive episode.

**B. [ ]** Persistent unhappiness unrelated to reminders
> Mood that stays low regardless of reminders of the loss is more typical of depression.

**C. [✔]** Self-esteem that is generally preserved
> In grief, self-esteem is usually preserved, while depression often brings worthlessness.

**D. [ ]** Inability to anticipate any future pleasure
> Pervasive anhedonia and pessimism about the future are characteristic of a depressive episode.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR notes that in grief the predominant feelings are emptiness and loss, dysphoria tends to come in waves ("pangs") linked to reminders of the deceased, humor can coexist with pain, and self-esteem is usually preserved. In a major depressive episode, mood is persistently low and is often accompanied by worthlessness and self-loathing.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Major Depressive Disorder, note distinguishing grief from a major depressive episode

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 123. nce-s-clf-027 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis and disaster response · easy · In review

A counselor volunteers at a shelter the day after a tornado destroyed many homes. Survivors are shaken, tired, and worried about relatives. Which approach is MOST appropriate in this early phase?

**A. [ ]** Lead a required group debriefing on each survivor's experience
> Mandatory single-session debriefing is not recommended and may increase distress for some survivors.

**B. [ ]** Give each survivor a structured PTSD diagnostic interview
> Most survivors recover naturally; diagnostic interviews this early are premature and intrusive.

**C. [ ]** Start trauma-focused exposure work with the survivors who are upset
> Trauma-focused therapy is for persistent symptoms after the acute phase, not as an immediate shelter intervention.

**D. [✔]** Offer psychological first aid for safety, comfort, and needs
> Psychological first aid promotes safety, calm, connection, and practical help, and is the recommended early response.

**Rationale:** In the immediate aftermath of a disaster, the recommended response is psychological first aid: ensuring safety, providing comfort, meeting practical needs, connecting people with loved ones and supports, and screening for those needing more help. Routine single-session psychological debriefing is not recommended for preventing PTSD.

**References:** NICE guidelines — NG116 Post-traumatic stress disorder: psychologically focused debriefing not offered

**⚑ Check:** No PFA-specific source on the approved list; NICE NG116 supports the no-debriefing point only.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 124. nce-s-clf-028 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · crisis and disaster response · medium · In review

A client calls the counselor sobbing after receiving an eviction notice. She says, "I just don't see the point of anything anymore." What should the counselor do FIRST?

**A. [✔]** Ask her directly about suicidal thoughts and intent
> A statement of hopelessness in crisis calls for direct assessment of suicidal ideation, intent, and plan before other steps.

**B. [ ]** Help her brainstorm options for finding new housing now
> Problem solving is useful later, but safety must be assessed first.

**C. [ ]** Reflect feelings and save risk questions for later
> Delaying risk assessment after a possible warning sign could miss imminent danger.

**D. [ ]** Give her the number of a local legal aid clinic
> A referral addresses the stressor but skips assessment of her immediate safety.

**Rationale:** In crisis intervention, ensuring safety comes before problem solving. Hopeless statements such as "I don't see the point" are possible warning signs, and asking directly about suicidal thoughts does not increase risk. Structured tools such as the C-SSRS guide questions about ideation, intent, plan, and behavior.

**References:** C-SSRS — Screener: suicidal ideation and intent questions

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 125. nce-s-clf-029 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · intimate partner violence · medium · In review

During the individual portion of a couples intake, one partner discloses that the other checks her phone, controls all the money, and shoved her last month. She says she is afraid of what will happen if he learns she told. What is the counselor's BEST next step?

**A. [ ]** Begin joint sessions on communication skills
> Conjoint work is contraindicated while coercive control and fear are present, as it can increase danger.

**B. [ ]** Raise her disclosure in the next joint session
> Revealing the disclosure to the partner could trigger retaliation.

**C. [ ]** Refer both partners to one anger management group
> Anger management does not address coercive control, and joint referral may compromise her safety.

**D. [✔]** Assess danger with her alone and plan for safety
> Individual risk assessment and safety planning come first when violence and fear are disclosed.

**Rationale:** When intimate partner violence involving fear and coercive control is disclosed, conjoint counseling is generally contraindicated because sessions can provoke retaliation. The counselor should meet with the person alone, assess lethality and danger, develop a safety plan, and connect her with advocacy resources while protecting the confidentiality of the disclosure.

**References:** ACA Code of Ethics — A.4.a Avoiding Harm

**⚑ Check:** IPV item cites general sources (ACA, Sue & Sue); no IPV-specific source on the approved list.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 126. nce-s-clf-030 · Social and Cultural Diversity · intimate partner violence · medium · In review

A recently immigrated client discloses that her husband hits her and threatens that she will be deported if she leaves him, since her visa depends on his sponsorship. She speaks limited English and has no family nearby. Which counselor response is MOST helpful?

**A. [ ]** Advise her to stay until her immigration status is secure
> This accepts the abuser's threat at face value and leaves her in danger without exploring legal protections.

**B. [✔]** Connect her with an immigration-informed IPV advocate
> Specialized advocates can explain protections available to abused immigrants and support culturally responsive safety planning.

**C. [ ]** Report the husband's conduct to immigration authorities
> This is not the counselor's role, breaches confidentiality, and may put the client at greater risk.

**D. [ ]** Suggest couples counseling to ease conflict at home now
> Couples counseling is contraindicated while there is ongoing violence and threats.

**Rationale:** Abusers of immigrant partners often use immigration status as a tool of control. Counselors should recognize this cultural and structural vulnerability, prioritize safety, provide interpreter and language access, and link clients with advocates who understand immigration-related protections rather than offering legal advice themselves.

**References:** Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Immigrant and refugee clients: structural barriers and advocacy

**⚑ Check:** IPV item cites general sources; the key deliberately avoids specific immigration-law (VAWA) claims.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 127. nce-s-clf-031 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · easy · In review

During a session at an elementary school, a 7-year-old tells the school counselor that her stepfather hit her with a belt last night, and the counselor sees welts on her arm. What should the counselor do FIRST?

**A. [✔]** Make a report to child protective services as the law requires of mandated reporters
> Reasonable suspicion of abuse triggers a mandated report; the counselor reports promptly and lets child protective services investigate.

**B. [ ]** Call the stepfather to hear his account of what happened before deciding to file any report
> Contacting the alleged offender can put the child at risk and is not required before reporting; investigation belongs to CPS.

**C. [ ]** Examine and photograph all of the child's injuries so the evidence is complete before reporting
> Counselors do not conduct forensic examinations; doing so can retraumatize the child and is the investigator's role.

**D. [ ]** Schedule a follow-up meeting for next week to see whether new marks appear before reporting
> Waiting delays protection; the reporting duty arises at reasonable suspicion, which is already present.

**Rationale:** Counselors are mandated reporters in every U.S. state. The threshold is reasonable suspicion, not proof, and the report is made promptly to child protective services or law enforcement. The counselor's job is to report and support the child, not to investigate or confront the alleged perpetrator.

**References:** State law — Mandated reporting statutes for suspected child abuse and neglect · ACA Code of Ethics — B.2.a Serious and Foreseeable Harm and Legal Requirements

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 128. nce-s-clf-032 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · medium · In review

A 10-year-old client mentions that he is often home alone for whole weekends with little food in the house. He gives few details and changes the subject. A colleague advises the counselor not to report neglect until the boy gives a clear account. Which statement BEST reflects the reporting standard?

**A. [ ]** A report is required only after the child gives a clear and consistent disclosure
> Children often disclose partially; the law does not require a complete or consistent disclosure before reporting.

**B. [ ]** A report is required only after a parent has a chance to explain the circumstances
> Mandated reporters do not need to hear the caregiver's explanation first; that is part of the CPS investigation.

**C. [✔]** A report is required when there is reasonable suspicion; proof of neglect is not needed
> The legal threshold is reasonable cause to suspect abuse or neglect, which repeated lack of supervision and food can meet.

**D. [ ]** A report is required only once a supervisor independently confirms that neglect occurred
> Consulting a supervisor is good practice, but the individual duty to report does not wait for anyone to confirm the facts.

**Rationale:** Mandated reporting laws use a reasonable-suspicion standard. Counselors report what they have observed or been told and leave fact-finding to child protective services. Neglect, including inadequate supervision and failure to provide food, is reportable in the same way as physical abuse.

**References:** State law — Child abuse and neglect reporting statutes: reasonable cause to suspect standard

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 129. nce-s-clf-033 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

In Holland's theory, consistency describes how close the first two letters of a person's code sit on the RIASEC hexagon. Which Holland code shows the LOWEST consistency?

**A. [ ]** RIC (Realistic-Investigative)
> Realistic and Investigative are adjacent on the hexagon, so this code is highly consistent.

**B. [ ]** SEA (Social-Enterprising)
> Social and Enterprising are adjacent on the hexagon, so this code is highly consistent.

**C. [ ]** ECR (Enterprising-Conventional)
> Enterprising and Conventional are adjacent on the hexagon, so this code is highly consistent.

**D. [✔]** RSI (Realistic-Social)
> Realistic and Social sit opposite each other on the hexagon, the least consistent pairing.

**Rationale:** Holland arranged the six types in the order R-I-A-S-E-C around a hexagon. Adjacent types share the most in common and opposite types the least, so a code whose first two letters are opposite (R-S, I-E, A-C) has low consistency. Consistency is distinct from differentiation (how sharply one type stands out) and congruence (person-environment fit).

**References:** Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Holland's theory of types: consistency, differentiation, congruence

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 130. nce-s-clf-034 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · hard · In review

A 52-year-old machinist who expected to finish his career at one plant is laid off when it closes. He is now researching training programs, trying out new interests, and reconsidering what work means to him. Which concept from Super's life-span, life-space theory BEST describes this process?

**A. [✔]** Recycling through exploration tasks during a later life stage
> Super held that adults can recycle through earlier stage tasks, such as exploration, when a transition disrupts their career.

**B. [ ]** Circumscription of options that conflict with his social class
> Circumscription belongs to Gottfredson's theory and describes early elimination of options by sex type and prestige.

**C. [ ]** Planned happenstance that turns the chance layoff into learning
> Planned happenstance is Krumboltz's concept, not Super's, and describes using unplanned events, not a stage process.

**D. [ ]** Correspondence between his abilities and the reinforcers of work
> Correspondence is the core idea of the theory of work adjustment (Dawis and Lofquist), not of Super's stage model.

**Rationale:** Super's stages (growth, exploration, establishment, maintenance, disengagement) form a maxicycle across the life span, but people can recycle through minicycles of earlier stages at transitions such as job loss. A midlife worker re-exploring options is the classic example of recycling.

**References:** Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Super's life-span, life-space theory: maxicycle, minicycles and recycling

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 131. nce-s-clf-035 · Career Development · career and vocational concerns · medium · In review

A 7-year-old girl who loves building things tells her counselor that she cannot be an engineer because "that is a job for boys." In Gottfredson's theory, what is this child doing?

**A. [ ]** Compromising by settling for a job that is more accessible to her
> Compromise comes later, when people give up preferred options because of real-world barriers to access.

**B. [✔]** Circumscribing her options by ruling out jobs seen as wrong for her sex
> Ages 6 to 8 are Gottfredson's orientation to sex roles, when children eliminate jobs that seem wrong for their gender.

**C. [ ]** Showing low differentiation because her interests are not yet clear
> Differentiation is a Holland concept about how distinct a profile is; her interest in building is actually clear.

**D. [ ]** Crystallizing a vocational preference as the exploration stage begins
> Crystallization is a task in Super's exploration stage during adolescence, not a process of ruling options out in childhood.

**Rationale:** Gottfredson's theory of circumscription and compromise describes children progressively eliminating occupations: by size and power (ages 3-5), by sex type (ages 6-8), by social valuing or prestige (ages 9-13), and then by internal, unique self (14 and older). Options eliminated early are often never reconsidered, so counselors can help children revisit them.

**References:** Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Gottfredson's theory of circumscription and compromise: orientation to sex roles

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 132. nce-s-clf-036 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · couples and family concerns · easy · In review

In couples sessions, one partner regularly rolls her eyes, mocks her spouse's tone, and calls him "pathetic." According to Gottman's research, which of the Four Horsemen is this, and the single strongest predictor of divorce?

**A. [ ]** Criticism
> Criticism attacks the partner's character, but it lacks the superiority and disgust that mark contempt.

**B. [ ]** Stonewalling
> Stonewalling is emotional withdrawal from the interaction, not mockery or eye-rolling.

**C. [ ]** Defensiveness
> Defensiveness is self-protection through excuses or counterattack, not a display of disgust.

**D. [✔]** Contempt
> Eye-rolling, mockery and name-calling express contempt, which Gottman found to be the strongest predictor of divorce.

**Rationale:** Gottman's Four Horsemen are criticism, contempt, defensiveness and stonewalling. Contempt communicates superiority and disgust through sarcasm, mockery, eye-rolling and insults, and it was the single best predictor of divorce in his studies. The antidote is building a culture of appreciation and respect.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Marriage, couple and family counseling: Gottman's research on the Four Horsemen

**⚑ Check:** Confirm the Gladding section that covers Gottman's Four Horsemen.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 133. nce-s-clf-037 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · couples and family concerns · medium · In review

A newly formed stepfamily seeks counseling because the 13-year-old daughter openly defies her new stepfather's rules, and he responds by giving stricter punishments. Which recommendation is MOST consistent with research on stepfamily adjustment?

**A. [ ]** Have the stepfather take over discipline so the teen accepts him as the new head of the home
> Early stepparent discipline before a bond forms tends to increase conflict and resentment.

**B. [✔]** Have the biological parent lead discipline while the stepparent builds a warm relationship first
> Stepfamily research supports the biological parent handling discipline early while the stepparent focuses on connection.

**C. [ ]** Have both adults avoid setting any rules until the teen says she is ready to accept the stepfather
> Removing structure leaves the teen without limits and puts her in charge of the family's functioning.

**D. [ ]** Have the teen spend more time with her other parent until she is willing to follow the new rules
> Using contact with the other parent as leverage triangulates the teen and can deepen loyalty conflicts.

**Rationale:** Stepfamilies form after losses and bring loyalty binds. Stepparents gain authority gradually, after a relationship exists, so the biological parent usually remains the primary disciplinarian at first while the stepparent acts more like a supportive adult. The couple still agrees on house rules together.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Family counseling: blended families and stepparent roles

**⚑ Check:** Confirm the Gladding section that covers stepfamily discipline roles.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 134. nce-s-clf-038 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · couples and family concerns · easy · In review

A counselor is about to begin conjoint counseling with a married couple. Each partner may also call or meet with the counselor alone from time to time. What should the counselor establish at the outset?

**A. [ ]** A promise that any individual disclosure will stay sealed from the other partner
> Promising to hold secrets can trap the counselor in a hidden alliance and undermine the couple work.

**B. [ ]** An agreement that each partner will be assigned their own separate counselor
> Separate counselors are an option but do not answer how this counselor will handle the couple case.

**C. [✔]** A clear policy, agreed by both, on how information shared individually is handled
> Clarifying who the client is and how individual information will be handled is required at the outset of multiple-client work.

**D. [ ]** A rule that the counselor will refuse to speak to either partner outside sessions
> Some contact outside joint sessions can be appropriate; the issue is a clear policy, not a ban.

**Rationale:** When counseling more than one person who share a relationship, counselors clarify at the outset who the client is and the limits of confidentiality, including whether a no-secrets or limited-secrets policy applies. Both partners agree to that policy before individual contacts happen.

**References:** ACA Code of Ethics — A.8 Multiple Clients; B.4.b Couples and Family Counseling

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 135. nce-s-clf-039 · Human Growth and Development · developmental life transitions · medium · In review

A counselor uses Schlossberg's transition model with a 58-year-old woman who has just retired early to care for her spouse. Which set of factors does the model use to assess her resources for coping?

**A. [✔]** Situation, self, support and strategies
> Schlossberg's 4 S's are situation, self, support and strategies, used to weigh a person's coping resources.

**B. [ ]** Denial, anger, bargaining and acceptance
> These are stages from Kübler-Ross's grief model, not Schlossberg's factors for coping with a transition.

**C. [ ]** Growth, exploration, establishment and decline
> These are life stages in Super's career theory, not resources for coping with a transition.

**D. [ ]** Shock, withdrawal, adjustment and integration
> This is a generic phase sequence, not the four coping-resource factors of Schlossberg's model.

**Rationale:** Schlossberg defines a transition as any event or non-event that changes roles, relationships, routines and assumptions. Coping is assessed through the 4 S's: the situation (timing, control, concurrent stress), the self (personal and psychological resources), support (relationships and institutions) and strategies (ways of coping).

**References:** Sharf (Career Development Theory) — Adult career transitions: Schlossberg's transition model (4 S's)

**⚑ Check:** Confirm Sharf covers Schlossberg's 4 S model (adult transitions).

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 136. nce-s-clf-040 · Human Growth and Development · developmental life transitions · easy · In review

A 24-year-old client lives with his parents, has changed jobs and majors several times, and says he feels "not really a kid, but not quite an adult." He is still exploring identity, work and relationships. Which concept BEST fits this period?

**A. [ ]** Identity foreclosure
> Foreclosure is commitment without exploration; this client is exploring and has not committed.

**B. [ ]** Midlife transition
> Levinson's midlife transition occurs around age 40, not in the early twenties.

**C. [ ]** Generativity versus stagnation
> Erikson's generativity stage concerns middle adulthood and guiding the next generation.

**D. [✔]** Emerging adulthood
> Arnett described emerging adulthood (about ages 18 to 25) as a time of identity exploration and feeling in-between.

**Rationale:** Arnett proposed emerging adulthood as a distinct period, roughly ages 18 to 25 in industrialized societies, marked by identity exploration, instability, self-focus, feeling in-between and a sense of possibilities. Counselors can frame this exploration as developmentally typical rather than as failure to launch.

**References:** Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Early adulthood: Arnett's emerging adulthood

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 137. nce-s-clf-041 · Human Growth and Development · aging and older adults · easy · In review

A 79-year-old widower in assisted living spends sessions reviewing his life, some proud moments and some regrets, and asks whether his life "added up to anything." Which of Erikson's psychosocial stages is he working through?

**A. [ ]** Generativity versus stagnation
> Generativity is the middle-adulthood task of guiding the next generation and being productive.

**B. [ ]** Intimacy versus isolation
> Intimacy is the young-adult task of forming close, committed relationships.

**C. [✔]** Integrity versus despair
> Late adulthood centers on accepting one's life as meaningful (integrity) rather than feeling regret (despair).

**D. [ ]** Identity versus role confusion
> Identity versus role confusion is the adolescent task of forming a coherent sense of self.

**Rationale:** Erikson's final stage, integrity versus despair, involves looking back and accepting one's life as it was. Life review, described by Butler, is a structured way to help older adults integrate successes and regrets and move toward integrity.

**References:** Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Late adulthood: Erikson's ego integrity versus despair; life review

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 138. nce-s-clf-042 · Human Growth and Development · aging and older adults · medium · In review

An adult daughter brings her 74-year-old mother to counseling, worried about "early dementia." Over two months the mother has lost interest in her garden, sleeps poorly, and often says "I just can't remember anything anymore." She seems distressed by her memory lapses. What should the counselor do FIRST?

**A. [✔]** Screen for depression and refer for a medical evaluation of the memory complaints
> Depression in older adults can mimic cognitive decline, and a medical workup rules out treatable causes.

**B. [ ]** Tell the family that memory loss of this kind is a normal part of growing older
> Noticeable decline with mood change is not normal aging and should not be dismissed.

**C. [ ]** Begin memory-training exercises in sessions to slow the progression of dementia
> This assumes a dementia diagnosis that has not been made and skips assessment of depression.

**D. [ ]** Recommend that the daughter begin looking into memory-care residential placement
> Placement planning is premature without assessment and could cause harm if the cause is depression.

**Rationale:** Late-life depression often presents with memory and concentration complaints, sometimes called pseudodementia. Clues include recent onset with mood symptoms and a person who is distressed by and emphasizes their lapses. The counselor screens for depression (for example with the Geriatric Depression Scale) and coordinates a medical evaluation.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Major depressive disorder: diminished ability to think or concentrate; differential with major neurocognitive disorder

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 139. nce-s-clf-043 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · chronic illness and disability · easy · In review

A client who uses a wheelchair after a spinal cord injury says, "My body is not the problem. The problem is buildings without ramps and managers who assume I can't do the job." Which model of disability does her statement reflect?

**A. [ ]** Medical model
> The medical model locates disability in the individual's impairment, to be diagnosed and treated.

**B. [ ]** Moral model
> The moral model treats disability as a sign of sin or a test of character.

**C. [ ]** Rehabilitation model
> This model focuses on restoring individual function, still locating the problem in the person.

**D. [✔]** Social model
> The social model locates disability in environmental and attitudinal barriers, not in the person.

**Rationale:** The social model distinguishes impairment (a bodily difference) from disability (exclusion created by inaccessible environments and attitudes). Counselors working from it attend to barriers, advocacy and accommodation as well as the client's adjustment.

**References:** Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Counseling individuals with disabilities: medical versus social models of disability

**⚑ Check:** Confirm Sue & Sue's disability coverage; 'rehabilitation model' distractor is a looser term than medical/moral/social.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 140. nce-s-clf-044 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · chronic illness and disability · hard · In review

A 34-year-old client with type 1 diabetes often skips glucose checks and insulin because, she says, "thinking about it makes it too real." Her blood sugar control has worsened and she has had two ER visits. She reports no persistent low mood, worry about having other illnesses, or other psychiatric symptoms. Which DSM-5-TR diagnosis BEST fits?

**A. [ ]** Illness anxiety disorder of the care-avoidant type
> Illness anxiety involves preoccupation with having or getting a serious illness, which she does not report.

**B. [✔]** Psychological factors affecting other medical conditions
> Her avoidance is a psychological factor that worsens a diagnosed medical condition, with no other mental disorder.

**C. [ ]** Somatic symptom disorder with a persistent course
> Somatic symptom disorder requires distressing physical symptoms with excessive related thoughts, not avoidance of care.

**D. [ ]** Factitious disorder imposed on self, with recurrent episodes
> Factitious disorder involves deliberately falsifying or inducing illness, which is not described here.

**Rationale:** Psychological factors affecting other medical conditions applies when psychological or behavioral factors, such as denial or poor adherence, adversely affect a medical condition and are not better explained by another mental disorder. It is listed with somatic symptom and related disorders but does not require distressing somatic symptoms.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Psychological Factors Affecting Other Medical Conditions: criteria A-B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 141. nce-s-clf-045 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · medium · In review

A bisexual client has never experienced overt harassment, yet she hides her identity at work, constantly scans for signs of rejection, and says part of her believes something is wrong with her. In Meyer's minority stress model, these experiences are BEST described as which kind of stressors?

**A. [ ]** Distal stressors from discrimination
> Distal stressors are external events such as harassment or discrimination, which she has not experienced.

**B. [ ]** General life stressors shared by all
> These stressors are tied specifically to her stigmatized identity, not common to everyone.

**C. [✔]** Proximal stressors tied to stigma
> Concealment, expected rejection and internalized stigma are Meyer's proximal (internal) minority stressors.

**D. [ ]** Coping resources within the community
> Community connection is a protective factor in the model, not a source of stress.

**Rationale:** Meyer's model separates distal stressors (external prejudice events, discrimination, violence) from proximal stressors (expectations of rejection, concealment and internalized homophobia or biphobia). Both add to the general stress everyone faces, and community connection and coping can buffer their effects on mental health.

**References:** Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Counseling LGBTQ individuals: minority stress

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 142. nce-s-clf-046 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · medium · In review

The parents of a 16-year-old who recently came out as gay ask a counselor to "help him become straight." The teen does not want to change his orientation. How should the counselor respond?

**A. [✔]** Decline to attempt orientation change and offer support for the teen and family
> Orientation change efforts lack scientific support and carry risk of harm; family support work is appropriate.

**B. [ ]** Agree to the goal because parents hold legal authority over a minor's treatment
> Parental consent does not make a harmful, unsupported technique ethical to use.

**C. [ ]** Refer the family to a provider who specializes in sexual orientation change work
> Referring for a harmful practice still facilitates it and does not protect the client.

**D. [ ]** Explore with the teen whether his orientation might be a phase that will soon pass
> Framing his identity as a phase invalidates it and reflects the parents' goal, not his own.

**Rationale:** The ACA Code bars techniques with substantial evidence of harm even when requested, and sexual orientation change efforts fall in that category. The counselor can decline that goal while still helping the family: providing education, supporting the parents' reactions and reducing family rejection, which is a strong risk factor for LGBTQ+ youth.

**References:** ACA Code of Ethics — C.7.a Scientific Basis for Treatment; C.7.c Harmful Practices

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 143. nce-s-clf-047 · Social and Cultural Diversity · acculturation and immigration stress · easy · In review

A man who immigrated from Vietnam 15 years ago has stopped speaking Vietnamese, no longer observes family traditions, and says he identifies only with mainstream American culture. Which of Berry's acculturation strategies does this describe?

**A. [ ]** Integration
> Integration keeps the heritage culture while also taking part in the host culture.

**B. [✔]** Assimilation
> Assimilation means giving up heritage culture and adopting the host culture.

**C. [ ]** Separation
> Separation keeps the heritage culture while avoiding the host culture.

**D. [ ]** Marginalization
> Marginalization means little connection to either the heritage or host culture.

**Rationale:** Berry's model crosses two questions: is the heritage culture maintained, and is contact with the host society sought? Yes and yes is integration; no and yes is assimilation; yes and no is separation; no and no is marginalization.

**References:** Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Acculturation: Berry's four acculturation strategies

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 144. nce-s-clf-048 · Social and Cultural Diversity · acculturation and immigration stress · hard · In review

A refugee teen has drifted away from her parents' language and customs but also feels unwelcome and isolated among peers at her new school. In Berry's framework, which strategy does this reflect, and how does it generally compare with the others in acculturative stress?

**A. [ ]** Separation, generally tied to the lowest acculturative stress
> Separation keeps heritage culture; she has moved away from hers, and integration, not separation, is lowest in stress.

**B. [ ]** Integration, generally tied to the highest acculturative stress
> She does not hold both cultures, and integration is usually associated with the least stress.

**C. [ ]** Assimilation, generally tied to the lowest acculturative stress
> She has not joined the host culture, and assimilation is not the lowest-stress strategy.

**D. [✔]** Marginalization, generally tied to the highest acculturative stress
> Losing ties to both cultures is marginalization, which Berry found carries the greatest acculturative stress.

**Rationale:** In Berry's research, integration is generally associated with the best adaptation and least acculturative stress, while marginalization, low engagement with both cultures, is associated with the most. Assimilation and separation fall in between. Counselors can help marginalized clients rebuild connection to one or both cultures.

**References:** Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Acculturation and acculturative stress: Berry's model

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 145. nce-s-clf-049 · Social and Cultural Diversity · spiritual and religious concerns · easy · In review

A devout client feels intense guilt about her divorce because of her faith community's teachings. The counselor is not religious and privately views the teachings as harmful. Which approach is MOST appropriate?

**A. [ ]** Encourage her to question the teachings that are leading her to feel guilty
> Steering her away from her beliefs imposes the counselor's values on the client.

**B. [✔]** Explore her guilt within her own faith framework and the resources it offers
> Working within the client's belief system respects her values and can draw on her faith as a resource.

**C. [ ]** Refer her to clergy because religious concerns fall outside counseling scope
> Spiritual concerns are within counseling scope; referral based on the topic alone is not warranted.

**D. [ ]** Disclose the counselor's own views so she can weigh both sides of the issue
> Sharing personal views on her religion serves the counselor and risks pressuring the client.

**Rationale:** Counselors avoid imposing their own values, attitudes and beliefs and respect the diversity of clients' spiritual lives. Exploring guilt within the client's framework, including forgiveness or grace in her tradition, honors her worldview and can turn faith into a source of coping.

**References:** ACA Code of Ethics — A.4.b Personal Values

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 146. nce-s-clf-050 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · self-injury · medium · In review

A 15-year-old discloses repeated nonsuicidal self-injury and says, "When everything builds up, it is the only thing that makes the feeling stop." Which function of self-injury does her statement MOST clearly reflect?

**A. [ ]** A wish to end her life that has not yet been spoken aloud
> NSSI by definition lacks suicidal intent; her words describe emotional relief, not a wish to die.

**B. [ ]** A bid to get attention from peers and adults around her
> NSSI is often hidden; attributing it to attention-seeking is a common, inaccurate assumption here.

**C. [✔]** Relief from intense negative emotion (affect regulation)
> Affect regulation, gaining relief from overwhelming feelings, is the most common reported function of NSSI.

**D. [ ]** Self-punishment for having broken a rule in her family
> Self-punishment is a possible function, but she describes stopping a feeling, not punishing herself.

**Rationale:** Research consistently finds that the most common function of nonsuicidal self-injury is affect regulation: obtaining relief from a negative feeling or cognitive state. DSM-5-TR lists this expectation in its proposed criteria for nonsuicidal self-injury disorder. Treatment builds alternative regulation skills, as in DBT.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Conditions for Further Study: Nonsuicidal Self-Injury Disorder, criterion B

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 147. nce-s-clf-051 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · self-injury · medium · In review

A college student shows the counselor healed marks on her forearm and says she has hurt herself during stressful weeks for two years. She denies wanting to die. Which counselor response is BEST?

**A. [ ]** Ask her to sign a no-harm contract before agreeing to continue in counseling
> No-harm contracts lack evidence of benefit and can shut down honest disclosure.

**B. [ ]** Arrange an inpatient admission because self-injury signals imminent danger
> NSSI without suicidal intent or other acute risk does not by itself call for hospitalization.

**C. [✔]** Respond calmly, explore what the behavior does for her, and assess suicide risk
> A calm, curious stance keeps her engaged, and NSSI still raises suicide risk, so risk is assessed.

**D. [ ]** Avoid discussing the marks so that attention does not reinforce the behavior
> Ignoring the disclosure misses assessment and can feel dismissive, discouraging further honesty.

**Rationale:** A respectful, nonjudgmental response to self-injury builds trust and allows functional assessment. NSSI is distinct from a suicide attempt but is a strong risk factor for later suicidal behavior, so counselors also assess suicidal ideation and behavior directly, for example with the C-SSRS.

**References:** C-SSRS — Suicidal behavior section: distinguishing nonsuicidal self-injurious behavior from suicide attempts

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 148. nce-s-clf-052 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · sleep-wake concerns · medium · In review

A counselor is using cognitive behavioral therapy for insomnia (CBT-I) with a client who lies awake in bed for hours most nights. Which instruction is part of the stimulus control component?

**A. [ ]** Avoid caffeine after noon and keep the bedroom cool, dark and quiet
> These are sleep hygiene recommendations, a separate and weaker component of insomnia care.

**B. [ ]** Limit time in bed to the hours actually slept, then extend it slowly
> This describes sleep restriction, a different CBT-I component that consolidates sleep.

**C. [ ]** Practice progressive muscle relaxation for twenty minutes at bedtime
> Relaxation training is a separate CBT-I component aimed at lowering arousal.

**D. [✔]** Get out of bed if unable to sleep and return only once feeling sleepy
> Stimulus control re-links the bed with sleep by leaving it when awake and returning when sleepy.

**Rationale:** Stimulus control therapy aims to restore the bed and bedroom as cues for sleep: go to bed only when sleepy, use the bed only for sleep and sex, leave the bed when unable to sleep, keep a fixed wake time and avoid daytime naps. It is combined with sleep restriction, cognitive work and education in CBT-I, the recommended first-line treatment for chronic insomnia.

**References:** NICE guidelines — Insomnia: CBT-I as first-line treatment for long-term insomnia

**⚑ Check:** NICE insomnia guidance is mainly the Clinical Knowledge Summary; citation placement is loose.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 149. nce-s-clf-053 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · sleep-wake concerns · easy · In review

A client reports difficulty staying asleep, with daytime fatigue that affects her work. The problem is not explained by a medical condition, substance or other sleep disorder. According to DSM-5-TR, how often and for how long must the sleep difficulty occur to meet criteria for insomnia disorder?

**A. [✔]** At least 3 nights a week for at least 3 months
> DSM-5-TR requires the difficulty at least 3 nights per week, present for at least 3 months.

**B. [ ]** At least 5 nights a week for at least 2 weeks
> This is far shorter than required; two weeks fits a brief, situational sleep problem.

**C. [ ]** At least 1 night a week for at least 6 months
> One night a week is below the frequency threshold for insomnia disorder.

**D. [ ]** At least 4 nights a week for at least 1 month
> One month falls short of the 3-month duration; briefer insomnia is coded as other specified insomnia disorder.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR insomnia disorder requires dissatisfaction with sleep quantity or quality (trouble initiating sleep, maintaining sleep or early waking) occurring at least 3 nights per week for at least 3 months, despite adequate opportunity for sleep, with clinically significant distress or impairment.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Insomnia Disorder: criteria C and D (frequency and duration)

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 150. nce-s-clf-054 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · psychopharmacology basics for counselors · hard · In review

A client with bipolar I disorder who takes lithium had a stomach virus with vomiting for two days. In session she has a coarse hand tremor, seems confused, and walks unsteadily. What should the counselor do?

**A. [ ]** Reassure her that tremor is a common side effect and continue the planned session
> A mild tremor can occur on lithium, but confusion and unsteady gait after dehydration suggest toxicity.

**B. [ ]** Suggest she skip her next lithium dose until she feels better, then call her prescriber
> Advising medication changes is outside counselor scope, and the symptoms need urgent evaluation now.

**C. [✔]** Arrange immediate medical evaluation because the symptoms may signal lithium toxicity
> Dehydration can raise lithium levels; confusion, coarse tremor and ataxia warrant urgent medical care.

**D. [ ]** Explore whether anxiety about her illness is causing her shaking and trouble focusing
> Attributing these signs to anxiety risks missing a medical emergency.

**Rationale:** Lithium has a narrow therapeutic range and requires regular blood-level monitoring. Vomiting, diarrhea, dehydration and some medications can raise levels into the toxic range, producing coarse tremor, confusion, ataxia and gastrointestinal symptoms.
