### 193. nce-s-clf-097 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · couples and family concerns · easy · In review

During a heated discussion in couples counseling, a husband pauses, smiles, and says, "Okay, I'm getting defensive. Can I try saying that again?" His wife's tone softens. In Gottman's research, what is the husband's statement called?

**A. [ ]** Stonewalling
> Stonewalling is withdrawing from the interaction, one of the Four Horsemen; he is re-engaging, not shutting down.

**B. [✔]** A repair attempt
> A repair attempt is any statement or action that de-escalates conflict and keeps negativity from spiraling.

**C. [ ]** A harsh startup
> A harsh startup is opening a discussion with criticism or contempt; his comment de-escalates.

**D. [ ]** Flooding
> Flooding is physiological overwhelm during conflict; his calm self-correction shows the opposite.

**Rationale:** Gottman found that stable couples argue too, but they make and accept repair attempts, such as humor, apologies or requests to start over, that stop negativity from escalating. Whether repairs succeed depends largely on the strength of the couple's friendship. Counselors teach partners to offer and recognize repair attempts.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Couples counseling: Gottman's research on repair attempts and the Four Horsemen

**⚑ Check:** Gottman's own texts are not on the approved source list; confirm the cited Gladding edition covers repair attempts.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 194. nce-s-clf-098 · Social and Cultural Diversity · spiritual and religious concerns · medium · In review

A 20-year-old college student raised in a close-knit congregation tells her counselor she has begun questioning teachings she once accepted without thought. She is reading widely, testing beliefs against her own reasoning, and wants a faith she has chosen for herself. Which of Fowler's stages of faith does this BEST describe?

**A. [ ]** Synthetic-conventional
> Synthetic-conventional faith conforms to the beliefs of one's group without critical examination, which she is now moving beyond.

**B. [ ]** Mythic-literal
> Mythic-literal faith, typical of school-age children, takes stories and symbols literally.

**C. [✔]** Individuative-reflective
> This stage involves critically examining inherited beliefs and taking personal responsibility for one's own commitments.

**D. [ ]** Conjunctive
> Conjunctive faith, usually in midlife or later, embraces paradox and other traditions after the reflective stage.

**Rationale:** Fowler proposed stages of faith development: primal (undifferentiated), intuitive-projective, mythic-literal, synthetic-conventional, individuative-reflective, conjunctive and universalizing. The shift from synthetic-conventional to individuative-reflective often occurs in late adolescence or young adulthood, when people move from group-held belief to a self-examined faith. Counselors can normalize this questioning rather than treat it as a crisis.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Spirituality in counseling: Fowler's stages of faith development

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 195. nce-s-clf-099 · Social and Cultural Diversity · spiritual and religious concerns · hard · In review

After a long illness in his family, a 63-year-old deacon says he has lost his sense that God listens and feels unmoored from his faith. He is sad at times but sleeps, eats and works normally, and his distress is in proportion to his experience. He meets criteria for no mental disorder. How should the counselor document the focus of treatment?

**A. [ ]** Adjustment disorder with depressed mood after the stressor
> Adjustment disorder requires distress out of proportion to the stressor or significant impairment; neither is present.

**B. [ ]** Persistent depressive disorder, given his low periods
> He has occasional sadness with normal functioning, not depressed mood most days for two years.

**C. [ ]** No code, since spiritual concerns fall outside counseling
> Spiritual concerns can be a legitimate focus of counseling and have a code for documentation.

**D. [✔]** Religious or spiritual problem as a focus of attention
> DSM-5-TR lists religious or spiritual problem (Z65.8) for distress such as loss or questioning of faith that is a focus of care.

**Rationale:** DSM-5-TR includes Religious or Spiritual Problem (Z65.8) among Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention. It covers distressing experiences such as loss or questioning of faith, conversion, or conflicts with a religious institution. It lets counselors document a legitimate focus of care without pathologizing a spiritual struggle that does not meet criteria for a mental disorder.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Other Conditions That May Be a Focus of Clinical Attention: Religious or Spiritual Problem (Z65.8)

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 196. nce-s-clf-100 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · medium · In review

A mother tells her counselor that her 11-year-old's new volunteer tutor is "amazing." He buys her daughter small gifts, offers rides so the mother can rest, texts the girl privately at night, and recently told her some of their talks are "just between us." Which feature MOST strongly suggests possible grooming?

**A. [✔]** Private contact paired with a request to keep secrets
> Grooming typically moves toward private access and secrecy, which isolate the child and lower the chance of disclosure.

**B. [ ]** His popularity with the parents in the neighborhood
> Groomers may win over parents, but being well liked is common among safe adults and is not a specific warning sign.

**C. [ ]** His strong interest in the girl's grades and reading
> Academic interest is expected of a tutor and is not by itself a warning sign.

**D. [ ]** His volunteering without pay for a nonprofit program
> Unpaid volunteering is ordinary and does not distinguish grooming from safe mentoring.

**Rationale:** Grooming is a gradual process in which an offender builds trust with a child and often the family, gives special attention or gifts, tests boundaries, increases private access and introduces secrecy. Secret-keeping and isolated contact are the most telling signs. Counselors educate parents about these patterns and follow mandated reporting law when abuse is reasonably suspected.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Child sexual abuse: grooming patterns and warning signs

**⚑ Check:** Confirm the cited source covers grooming; the concept itself is standard in child-protection literature.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 197. nce-s-clf-101 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · child abuse and neglect · easy · In review

A school counselor notices bruises on an active 6-year-old who plays rough sports at recess. Which bruise location should raise the GREATEST concern about possible physical abuse?

**A. [ ]** Scattered bruises over both shins and knees
> Shins and knees are bony areas that are commonly bruised in normal childhood play.

**B. [ ]** A bruise on the forehead after a playground fall
> The forehead is a common site of accidental injury in falls, especially with a matching account.

**C. [✔]** Bruises on the ears, cheeks and the upper back
> Ears, cheeks and the back are protected, soft areas rarely bruised by accident, so injuries there suggest inflicted harm.

**D. [ ]** Bruises on the elbows of both arms from falling
> Elbows are bony prominences that often take the impact of accidental falls.

**Rationale:** Accidental bruises in children usually appear over bony prominences such as the shins, knees, elbows and forehead. Bruises on soft or protected areas such as the ears, cheeks, neck, torso, back, buttocks or upper arms, or bruises in the shape of an object or hand, are more concerning for abuse. Reasonable suspicion triggers a mandated report.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — Recognizing physical indicators of child abuse · State law — Child abuse mandated reporting statutes: reasonable-suspicion standard

**⚑ Check:** Confirm the cited Gladding edition lists physical indicators of abuse; the injury-location pattern is standard pediatric guidance.

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 198. nce-s-clf-102 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · school and academic concerns · easy · In review

A middle school counselor is preparing a parent workshop on cyberbullying. Which feature MOST clearly sets cyberbullying apart from traditional in-person bullying?

**A. [ ]** It involves a real or perceived imbalance of power
> A power imbalance is part of the definition of all bullying, not a distinguishing feature of cyberbullying.

**B. [✔]** It can reach the child at home, at any hour, by many
> Online bullying follows children home around the clock and can be seen and shared by large audiences, often anonymously.

**C. [ ]** It is intentional and is repeated over a period of time
> Intent and repetition define bullying in general, both online and in person.

**D. [ ]** It harms the target's mood and school performance
> Both forms of bullying are linked to depression, anxiety and academic problems.

**Rationale:** Bullying is unwanted aggressive behavior that is intentional, repeated or likely to be repeated, and involves a power imbalance. Cyberbullying shares these features but differs in that it can occur at any time and place, reach a wide audience, persist as a permanent record, and allow anonymity, so home is no longer a refuge.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — School counseling: bullying and cyberbullying

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 199. nce-s-clf-103 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · school and academic concerns · medium · In review

For several weeks, two larger eighth graders have mocked a smaller classmate, taken his lunch, and posted edited photos of him. The principal asks the school counselor to hold a peer mediation session so the three boys can "work it out together." What is the counselor's BEST response?

**A. [ ]** Agree, and set clear ground rules so each boy can share equally
> Ground rules do not remove the power imbalance that makes mediation unsafe and unfair for a bullied student.

**B. [ ]** Agree, but ask the target to suggest ways he could avoid the boys
> This places responsibility on the target and implies he shares blame for being bullied.

**C. [ ]** Decline, and leave the matter to discipline by the administration
> Discipline may be needed, but the counselor still has a role in supporting the target and working with the students who bully.

**D. [✔]** Explain why mediation fits poorly and meet with the boys separately
> Because bullying involves a power imbalance, separate meetings that support the target and hold the others accountable are preferred.

**Rationale:** Peer mediation assumes two parties with roughly equal power and shared responsibility for a conflict. Bullying is repeated, intentional aggression with a power imbalance, so mediation can retraumatize the target and send the message that both are to blame. Recommended practice is to support the target, address the behavior of those who bully separately, and involve families and school-wide prevention.

**References:** Gladding (Counseling: A Comprehensive Profession) — School counseling: responding to bullying; limits of peer mediation

**⚑ Check:** Confirm the cited source states that peer mediation is not recommended for bullying (a position also taken by federal StopBullying guidance).

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 200. nce-s-clf-104 · Human Growth and Development · aging and older adults · easy · In review

A counselor reviewing a new referral for a 77-year-old with low mood, poor sleep and withdrawal thinks, "Anyone her age would feel down. Therapy probably won't change much at this point." What does the counselor's reaction BEST reflect?

**A. [✔]** Ageist bias that risks undertreating treatable depression
> Treating depression as a normal part of aging, and assuming older adults cannot change, is ageism and leads to undertreatment.

**B. [ ]** Sound clinical judgment about late-life treatment outcomes
> Older adults respond well to psychotherapy for depression; the assumption is not supported.

**C. [ ]** Appropriate humility about his limits with older clients
> Humility would lead to consultation or training, not a belief that therapy is futile.

**D. [ ]** A correct view of depression as part of normal aging
> Depression is common in later life but is not a normal part of aging.

**Rationale:** Ageism includes stereotypes that older adults are rigid, unable to change, or naturally depressed. In clinicians it can show up as therapeutic nihilism and missed diagnoses. Depression is not a normal part of aging, and evidence-based therapies such as CBT and problem-solving therapy work well for older adults.

**References:** Berk (Development Through the Lifespan) — Late adulthood: ageism, stereotypes of aging and late-life depression

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 201. nce-s-clf-105 · Counseling and Helping Relationships · aging and older adults · medium · In review

An 84-year-old client mentions that her nephew, who now manages her finances, has her sign blank checks, and that her utilities were shut off last month. She seems anxious when he is mentioned and asks the counselor not to "make trouble." What should the counselor do?

**A. [ ]** Wait to act until she gives proof that funds were taken
> Reporting standards rest on reasonable suspicion; counselors do not need proof before acting.

**B. [ ]** Call the nephew to ask him to explain how he uses the money
> Confronting a suspected abuser can raise the risk to the client and breaches confidentiality.

**C. [✔]** Follow state law and report suspected exploitation to APS
> Financial exploitation of an older adult is reportable abuse in many states; counselors follow their state's law and report to Adult Protective Services.

**D. [ ]** Respect her wish and only document what she has disclosed
> Her wishes matter, but many states mandate reports of suspected elder abuse regardless of consent.

**Rationale:** Elder abuse includes physical, emotional, sexual and financial abuse and neglect; perpetrators are often family members or trusted caregivers. Signs of financial exploitation include unpaid bills despite adequate income and unusual control of money. Reporting laws vary, so counselors know their state's requirements, report suspected abuse to Adult Protective Services when required, and involve the client as much as possible.

**References:** State law — Elder and vulnerable adult abuse reporting statutes (requirements vary by state) · ACA Code of Ethics — B.2.a Serious and Foreseeable Harm and Legal Requirements

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 202. nce-s-clf-106 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · hard · In review

A 29-year-old nonbinary client, assigned male at birth, has lived in line with their gender identity for years and feels settled and content with it. They seek counseling for work stress after a promotion. Which statement about a gender dysphoria diagnosis is MOST accurate?

**A. [ ]** It applies, since gender identity differs from assigned sex
> Incongruence alone is not a disorder; DSM-5-TR also requires clinically significant distress or impairment.

**B. [✔]** It does not apply without related distress or impairment
> Gender dysphoria requires clinically significant distress or impairment linked to the incongruence, which this client does not report.

**C. [ ]** It applies, since the incongruence has lasted over 6 months
> Duration is only part of the criteria; distress or impairment is also required.

**D. [ ]** It does not apply, since nonbinary identities are excluded
> DSM-5-TR criteria include a desire to be or be treated as an alternative gender, which covers nonbinary identities.

**Rationale:** In DSM-5-TR, gender dysphoria requires a marked incongruence between experienced or expressed gender and assigned gender lasting at least 6 months, plus clinically significant distress or impairment. The diagnosis centers on distress, not identity; being transgender or nonbinary is not itself a mental disorder. Counselors avoid diagnosing identity and focus on the client's presenting concerns.

**References:** DSM-5-TR — Gender Dysphoria in adolescents and adults: Criterion A duration and Criterion B distress or impairment

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________

---

### 203. nce-s-clf-107 · Social and Cultural Diversity · LGBTQ+ clients and minority stress · easy · In review

In the third session, a 17-year-old client tells the counselor that he is transgender, goes by Eli, and uses he/him pronouns. He has not told his parents. Which counselor response is MOST affirming?

**A. [ ]** Ask him to wait a few months before changing his name
> Delaying use of his name invalidates his identity and can weaken trust in the relationship.

**B. [ ]** Use his legal name in session until his parents know
> Withholding his name in session is not affirming; safety concerns apply to records and outside contacts, not to how the counselor addresses him.

**C. [ ]** Explore whether this is a phase before using his name
> Treating his identity as a phase to be tested is not affirming practice.

**D. [✔]** Use Eli and he/him, and ask where it is safe to do so
> Affirming care uses the client's name and pronouns and plans together where they are used, such as in records or with family, to avoid outing him.

**Rationale:** Affirming counseling with transgender clients means using their chosen name and pronouns, treating gender identity as valid, and collaborating on safety, including when names appear in records or communications that parents or others may see. This respects the client's autonomy and protects against unwanted disclosure.

**References:** ACA Code of Ethics — A.1.a Primary Responsibility; C.5 Nondiscrimination · Sue & Sue (Counseling the Culturally Diverse) — Counseling LGBTQ+ individuals: affirming practice

☐ Approve  ☐ Revise  ☐ Retire   Notes: ____________________
